**Claude:** All tests pass: **481 passed, 3 known limitations**. The third known limitation is the new `dt` test. It's marked `strict`, so if the model ever does stop depending on `dt`, that test will fail and tell us.

**Bugs fixed**
- **Seven guardrail bugs.** The most important ones:
  - `herding="no"` switched herding **on**;
  - `max_mispricing=0` turned the safety guard **off**;
  - an empty ensemble returned NaN silently, which read as "typical".

  Each one now stops with a clear error, and each has a test.
- **The switching cap.** The model used `min(1, rate)`, which made results depend on the step size. It now uses the exact formula `1 − e^(−rate)`, with `dt = 0.01`, and the model is refit.
- **Fig 4 picks its typical history automatically.** It had seed 604 hard-coded, which would have stopped being the typical one after the refit. It now uses seed 616.
- **The breaker test** got wilder settings so it still has a −20% day to check. The old `typical.py` analysis now runs on the current calibration.

**What changed in your results (calibration v3)**

| | Before (v2) | Now (v3) |
|---|---|---|
| Fit to the S&P (mismatch, lower is better) | 11.3 | **8.9** |
| Measures typical on held-out runs | 9 of 10 | **8 of 10** (reversal weak spot shows on 2 measures) |
| Tail α vs S&P 2.92 | 3.48 | **2.94** |
| Biggest model day | ~21 std | **~12 std** (Black Monday was 23) |
| Breakers lower the worst day | 14 of 20 histories | **20 of 20**, but only by ~5% |
| Crowded-market surprise overshoot (mean) | 1.07× | **1.10×** |

Your main conclusions still hold:
- Herding alone creates the fat tail.
- With no chartists you get a bell curve.
- Crashes come from crowding into trend-following.
- Breakers spread crashes out rather than stop them.
- Surprises hurt most in crowded markets.

The model no longer produces Black-Monday-sized days, and `RESULTS.md` now says so. **Known limitation 6** records that `dt` is part of the model's definition, and that simulating one trader switch at a time would remove it.

**Exercise:** under the new calibration, the model's kurtosis is about 10 and the S&P's is 25. Yet the tail exponents nearly match (2.94 vs 2.92). Using Fig 2, explain how both can be true at once. Hint: look at which part of the curve α measures and which part kurtosis is most sensitive to.

**Claude:** I read the whole paper and checked our project against it. **I haven't changed anything.** The only new files are the extracted text and a check script, both in `scratch/`, which git ignores.

## What the paper can and can't check
The PDF is the 4-page Nature letter. It describes the model in words, and says the equations and parameter values are in its Supplementary Information, which this PDF doesn't include. So I could check **what each part does**, but not exact formulas or numbers.

## ✅ What matches the paper
- **Three groups:** fundamentalists, plus optimistic and pessimistic noise traders. Our "chartists" are the paper's "noise traders".
- **How switching works:** the chance of a switch in a small time step is *v·e^U·Δt*. Our `1 − e^(−rate)` is the exact form of that, and the two agree when Δt is small.
- **Optimist ↔ pessimist switching:** depends on the majority opinion plus the price trend. That's our `a1` and `a2` terms.
- **Noise trader ↔ fundamentalist switching:** depends on comparing profits.
  - Optimists earn short-term capital gains.
  - Pessimists earn the alternative investment's rate minus the price change.
  - Fundamentalists' arbitrage profit is discounted. That's our `U21`/`U22`.
- **Price:** set by excess demand. That's noise traders' net count times a fixed trade size, plus fundamentalists reacting to the relative gap between price and value.
- **News:** the log-changes of the fundamental value are Gaussian with constant variance.
- **Crash mechanism:** the paper says volatility rises when the share of noise traders gets near a **critical value**, then fundamentalists stabilize the market again. That matches Fig 4. It also explains the `dt` problem from last session: the calibrated market sits right at that critical point, which is why tiny timing details matter.

## ⚠️ Problems I found (waiting for your approval)
1. **The "1999 paper settings" label is wrong.** The Nature letter gives no parameter values. Our defaults say "after Lux & Marchesi (2000)", and we changed several of them ourselves (`beta`, `tc`, `gamma`, `a2`). Fix: relabel them "default settings (based on Lux & Marchesi 2000)" in `main.py`, `make_figures.py`, `RESULTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`. Changes only words.
2. **The discount factor `s` must be below 1.** The paper says fundamentalists' profits are discounted by a factor less than 1. Our search range allows `s` up to **2.0**, and the old v2 calibration used 1.18, which broke this rule. The current value, 0.50, is allowed but sits at the bottom edge. Fix: narrow the range to 0.5–1.0. The current calibration is already inside it, so no refit is needed.
3. **Our trend is smoothed, and the paper's isn't.** The paper uses the *actual* price trend; ours averages over about the last day (`tau = 0.93`). This isn't listed in the `RESULTS.md` limitations. Fix: add it there. Changes only text.
4. **New finding: weekly returns get fatter-tailed, the opposite of the paper.** The paper shows that over longer horizons, returns look more like a bell curve. The S&P does exactly that, but our model gets *more* extreme at 5 days before calming down:

   | Kurtosis at horizon (days) | 1 | 5 | 15 | 25 |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | S&P 500 | 25.2 | 5.3 | 3.2 | 3.3 |
   | Calibrated model | 10.5 | **17.3** | 1.4 | 0.4 |

   The likely cause is that our crashes build up over several days in a row. I don't see a code bug behind it, but it's a real mismatch. I'd like to investigate it, then add it as a test and a documented limitation.
5. **The paper's own measures.** I measured everything the paper's way:

   | Measure | Paper | S&P 500 | Our model |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | Tail α (paper's method: line fit on the biggest 30% of days) | 2.64 | 2.35 | 2.51 |
   | H for raw returns (0.5 = no memory) | 0.48 | 0.49 | **0.41** |
   | H for return sizes (above 0.5 = volatility memory) | 0.85 | 0.89 | 0.94 |

   H is a "memory" number from a method called DFA (detrended fluctuation analysis). Below 0.5 means moves tend to reverse; above 0.5 means they persist. Our 0.41 is the same 2-day-reversal weak spot we already know about. Everything else is close to the paper and the S&P. Proposed addition: put the paper's α method, H, and the multi-horizon check into `stats.py` with tests, and add a figure that reproduces the paper's Figs 2–3. That would directly support your title, *"Reproducing the Lux–Marchesi Model"*.
6. **Two things I can't check from this paper.** The exact formulas and our price-setting rule: we move the price smoothly, while I believe the 2000 paper moves it one tick at a time at random, but I'm not certain. Checking either would need the Supplementary Information or the 2000 paper through CMU's library.

## 📄 About the PDF
The PDF says further reproduction is prohibited without permission, and your repo is public. So I **shouldn't** commit it to `paper/` as `CLAUDE.md` currently suggests. My suggestion: keep it in `paper/` locally, but add it to `.gitignore` so it never reaches GitHub.

Tell me which numbers to do (for example, "do 1, 2, 3 and the PDF"), and I'll explain each change as I make it.

## What the graphs show

**Fig 1 (price, returns, news over 4,000 steps)**
- The price follows the fundamental value closely the whole time.
- The bursts of volatility are **frequent and mild**: about four in 4,000 steps, with the biggest move only about 3–4 times a normal day.
- **Our calibrated model is different:** its bursts are **rare and violent**, around 12 std, with mispricing up to 29% and nearly 100% chartists (Fig 4). We tuned it to the S&P, which really does have rare huge days. So our model sits in a different regime from the one the paper shows. That's fine, but `RESULTS.md` should say so rather than imply we reproduce the paper's picture.
- A rough reading of panel a: the fundamental value wanders about 0.4–0.5% per step, roughly half our `sigma_news` of 1% per day. That's only an estimate from the graph.

**Fig 2 (tails at 1, 5, 15, 25 steps)**
- **This confirms problem 4.** In the paper, the 5-step curve sits clearly *below* the 1-step curve in the tail, and the 15- and 25-step curves are almost on the bell curve. In our model the 5-step returns are *fatter* than the 1-step ones. It's a real mismatch, not a quirk of the kurtosis number.
- The x-axis labels look misprinted: the second tick reads 10⁻¹ but should be bigger than 10⁰. So I can't read exact move sizes off this graph, only its shape.
- The paper's 2.64 comes from a line fit over the top 30% of days. On the graph, most of that range is the curved, bell-shaped middle, not the straight tail. So 2.64 isn't a pure tail estimate, which is why the same method gives the S&P 2.35 while our tail-only method gives 2.9. Comparing our numbers to 2.64 needs that caveat.

**Fig 3 (memory, H)**
- Both lines are straight from about 100 to 100,000 steps, so the paper simulated **at least 100,000 steps**. My H check used our 19,300-day histories and windows of 16–1,024 days, so it isn't a like-for-like comparison yet. A fair reproduction needs longer runs.

## Updated list (still waiting for your approval)
1. Relabel "1999 paper settings" as "default settings (based on Lux & Marchesi 2000)". Text only.
2. Narrow the `s` search range to 0.5–1.0, since the paper requires a factor below 1. No refit needed.
3. Document the smoothed trend (`tau`) as a difference from the paper. Text only.
4. **Investigate why our 5-day returns get fatter-tailed** when the paper's (and the S&P's) get thinner. Then add a test and a figure. Now confirmed by the paper's Fig 2.
5. **New: add a "reproduce the paper" figure.** It would match the paper's Figs 1–3 using the default settings and 100,000-step runs, with the paper's own α method and H, and state honestly where we match and where we don't.
6. **New: add to `RESULTS.md`** that our calibrated crashes are rarer and more violent than the paper's bursts, and that its 2.64 isn't a pure tail number. Text only.
7. Keep the PDF local only, via `.gitignore`, because of its copyright notice.

**Claude:** The app's PDF viewer works now, and it rendered all 4 pages directly. The pages match what I reviewed before, so nothing in my findings changes. Here's everything in one place.

# 1. What the paper says, in plain language

**The question:** real markets have **fat tails** (huge days happen far more often than a bell curve predicts) and **volatility clustering** (wild days come in bunches). Do these come from the news, or from how traders behave toward each other?

**The model:**
- **Fundamentalists** buy when the price is below the true value and sell when it's above.
- **Noise traders** (our "chartists") follow trends and each other. They split into **optimists**, who buy, and **pessimists**, who sell.
- **Switching:** in each small time step Δt, a trader switches group with probability *v·e^U·Δt*. Here *v* is how often traders reconsider, and *U* is the push toward switching.
  - **Optimist ↔ pessimist:** *U* combines the **majority mood** (are most noise traders optimistic?) and the **price trend**.
  - **Noise trader ↔ fundamentalist:** *U* compares **profits**. Optimists earn capital gains. Pessimists earn a safe interest rate minus the price change. Fundamentalists earn the gap between price and value, discounted by a factor below 1, because that profit only arrives later.
- **Price:** rises when buyers outnumber sellers. Noise traders' demand is their optimist–pessimist count times a fixed trade size. Fundamentalists' demand grows with how far the price is from value.
- **News:** the true value moves by plain bell-curve random steps, so the input has no fat tails and no clustering.

**What they found:**
- **Fig 1:** the price tracks the value closely, but its returns show bursts. The news doesn't.
- **Fig 2:** returns have a power-law tail with **α ≈ 2.64**. Summed over 5, 15 and 25 steps they turn back into a bell curve, just like real weekly and monthly returns.
- **Fig 3:** a memory measure called **H**. H = 0.5 means no memory, and above 0.5 means memory. The direction of returns has none (H ≈ 0.48); their size has strong memory (H ≈ 0.85).
- **Why:** there's a **critical share of noise traders**. Above it the market loses stability and volatility bursts; then fundamentalists see profit in the mispricing and calm it down. Physicists call this switching between calm and bursts **on-off intermittency**. They say it holds for many different parameter settings.

# 2. Our project checked against the paper

| Part | Matches the paper? |
|---|---|
| Three groups, Kirman-style switching | ✅ |
| Switching probability *v·e^U·Δt* | ✅ Ours is the exact version; it agrees when Δt is small |
| Opinion switching (mood + trend) | ✅ `a1`, `a2` |
| Strategy switching (profit comparison) | ✅ `U21`, `U22` |
| Discount factor below 1 | ⚠️ Current `s = 0.50` is fine, but the search allows up to 2.0, and the old v2 calibration used 1.18 |
| Price set by excess demand | ✅ But we use log prices and a smoothed trend (`tau`); the second isn't documented |
| Gaussian news | ✅ |
| Fat tails from traders, not news | ✅ The main result holds |
| Crashes from crowding into noise trading | ✅ Our Fig 4 is exactly the paper's critical-fraction story |
| Returns become bell-shaped over longer horizons | ❌ Our 5-day returns are *fatter* (kurtosis 17.3 vs 10.5 daily) |
| Turbulence is overcome quickly | ⚠️ Mostly (median 3 days), but some crowded episodes last up to 4 years |
| No memory in direction (H ≈ 0.48) | ⚠️ Ours is 0.41: the 2-day reversal weak spot |
| Volatility memory (H ≈ 0.85) | ✅ Ours is 0.94 and the S&P's 0.89, though our runs were shorter than the paper's |
| Tail α ≈ 2.64 (paper's method) | ✅ Ours is 2.51 and the S&P's 2.35. Their method also fits the bell-shaped middle, so it isn't a pure tail number |
| "1999 paper settings" label | ❌ The letter gives no parameter values. Ours come from the 2000 paper, partly changed by us |
| Exact formulas | ❓ They're in the Supplementary Information, which isn't in this PDF |

## The paper's political and economic standpoints
The paper takes **no political or policy position**: it never mentions government, regulation or the Fed. Its one standpoint is economic. It argues against the *efficient market hypothesis*, the idea that big price swings can only come from big news, while still saying the market is efficient *on average*. I measured its claims on our model:

| The paper's claim | Our model | Was it tested before? |
|---|---|---|
| The price equals the value on average | ✅ mean gap −0.0000 | partly |
| No persistent pathological mispricing | ✅ longest stretch over 10% off is 11 days | ❌ |
| Volatility rises near a critical share of noise traders | ✅ 0.67× to 2.97× | ❌ |
| Price direction is unpredictable | ⚠️ H = 0.44, a mild reversal | partly |
| Fat tails come from traders, not news | ✅ | ✅ |

The three never-tested claims now have reproducible scripts in `analysis/`. As I said I would, I did **not** add them as automated tests; tell me if you want that.

## What I changed
1. **Relabeled the "paper settings"** as "default settings" everywhere: the figures, `main.py`, `RESULTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`. The 1999 letter gives no parameter values.
2. **Limited `s` to 0.5–1.0**, since the paper requires a discount factor below 1, and added a test. The calibration was already inside this range and is unchanged.
3. **Documented the smoothed trend (`tau`)** as a difference from the paper.
4. **Weekly returns get fatter-tailed: I found the cause.** Our crashes are **slow-motion slides**. The day after a big move continues it (+0.31), while the S&P bounces back (−0.19). This is now a strict known-limitation test, shown in Fig 8 and explained in both write-ups. **Fixing it would change the model, so that's your decision.**
5. **New Fig 8** reproduces the paper's Figs 1–3. Highlight: our calibrated **H = 0.85 is exactly the paper's value** (S&P: 0.88). I also added the paper's measures to `stats.py`, with 13 tests built on known math. One of those tests caught that **even a pure bell curve scores α ≈ 3.7** with the paper's tail method.
6. **Updated `RESULTS.md`** to describe the different regime (rarer, more violent crashes than the paper's example) and gave it 3 new limitations.
7. **Moved the PDF to `paper/` and git-ignored it.** It had been sitting in your project's top folder, so the next commit would have published it.
8. ~~Homebrew~~ (done earlier).
9. **New Fig 9, robustness:** fat tails appear for **98%** of random settings, but long volatility memory for only **46%**. Herding makes fat tails almost automatically, while long memory needs particular settings.

## The new file: `KEY_POINTS.md`
It's a standalone guide for anyone new to the project. It defines every term, explains the model in plain words, then walks through 9 findings, each with its figure, how to read it, and the numbers. It also covers the paper's standpoint, the honest limitations, the sources, and commands to reproduce everything.

2. **Key Technical Concepts**
   - **Lux–Marchesi model**
     - Fundamentalists, plus optimist and pessimist chartists (noise traders); Kirman recruitment.
     - Switching probability is now `1-exp(-rate)`, the exact version of the paper's `v·e^U·Δt`.
     - Inputs: `U1` (opinion: `a1` mood + `a2` trend); `U21`/`U22` (profit comparison, discount `s` < 1); log prices; smoothed trend `tau`; floors; `dt = 0.01` is part of the model definition.
   - **The paper (Nature 397:498–500)**
     - Tail α 2.64, from a log-log regression on the top 30% of the CCDF.
     - DFA H: 0.48 for raw returns, 0.85 for |r|, 0.49–0.51 for news.
     - Aggregation crossover to a normal distribution at τ = 5, 15, 25.
     - A critical noise-trader fraction drives on-off intermittency.
     - Robust over an open set of parameters.
     - Argues against the Efficient Market Hypothesis, while saying markets are efficient on average. No government or regulatory content.
   - **New stats functions:** `aggregate`, `alpha_regression`, `dfa`, `hurst`.
   - **Methods:** held-out seeds; percentiles; paired runs; strict xfail for known limitations; golden-output regression test (`golden_v2.npz`); numba JIT; multiprocessing Pool (needs a `__main__` guard and a real script file, since stdin heredoc + Pool hangs).
   - **Infrastructure:** poppler/pdftoppm via Homebrew at `/opt/homebrew`. pypdf and sips were used for earlier PDF extraction; PIL composited the transparent PNGs onto white.

3. **Files and Code Sections**
   - **`herding/model.py`**
     - `_prob(rate)` returns `1.0 - np.exp(-max(0.0, rate))`. The docstring explains the bug fix replacing `min(1, rate)`.
     - `P` now has `dt=0.01`.
     - Comment above `P`: "Default settings, based on Lux & Marchesi (2000)... beta, tc, gamma and a2 were changed... NOT the paper's exact settings (the 1999 Nature letter gives no parameter values at all). Figures call them 'default settings'." Plus a comment that dt=0.01 is part of the model's definition.
     - Guardrails:
       - `_is_number(x)`;
       - `check_inputs` requires a bool `herding` and a numeric, finite `days`;
       - `check_breakers` requires a dict, numeric levels, and a finite `halt_minutes` > 0;
       - `check_shocks` requires a dict;
       - `simulate_full` requires `max_mispricing` to be None or a finite number > 0.
   - **`herding/stats.py`**
     - `acf` validates whole-number lags.
     - New functions:
       - `aggregate(r, tau)` returns sums of blocks of τ days and drops the leftover partial block.
       - `alpha_regression(r, top=0.30)` returns the negative slope of log ccdf vs log z over the top share.
       - `dfa(x, scales=(16..2048))` returns `(scales, F)` and raises if a scale exceeds len/4.
       - `hurst(x, scales)` returns the log-log slope.
   - **`herding/calibrate.py`**
     - `RANGES` now has `s=(0.5, 1.0)`, with a comment citing the paper's discount factor below 1.
     - `percentiles` and `representative` raise on empty input.
     - `calibrated_params.json` is now v3: dt 0.01, loss 8.93, s 0.5, a1 0.227, a2 2.106, a3 0.772, v1 3.753, v2 1.514, tc 0.032, gamma 0.432, beta 41.069, tau 0.928.
     - The old calibration is in `analysis/history/calibration_v2_dt002.json`.
   - **`herding/kirman.py`**
     - `check` rejects `eps + (1-delta)*2k(N-k)/(N(N-1)) > 1` at k = N//2.
   - **`herding/market_data.py`**

     - An `alpha_regression` Gaussian test assumed a +1 gap. The measured value is 3.7, so the test was corrected; the finding is documented.

     - A layout pattern match failed (the title was on one line, not two); redone with the right pattern.
     - Fig 7's ugly inline conditional was rewritten.
     - Fig 9's "46%" headline was misleading; it now reports tails and memory separately.

     - Fig 3's caption overstated "tracks the S&P"; reworded.

5. **Problem Solving**
   - **dt dependence:** reduced by the exact switching probability but not eliminated. The market maker and the trend term were ruled out. The cause is near-critical dynamics. Documented as a known limitation; Gillespie is future work.
   - **Aggregation mismatch explained:** crashes are slow-motion slides. Next-day correlation after a >3 std move is +0.31 vs the S&P's −0.19. Worst week: 4.5 of 5 days same sign. Worst week is 12 std vs a worst day of 12 (S&P: 8.5 vs 23).
   - **Paper checks with 100k-day runs:**

     | | α (regression) | H returns | H \|r\| | Kurtosis by horizon |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | Calibrated | 2.55 | 0.44 | 0.85 | 10.7 → 20.8 → 2.1 → 0.8 |
     | Default | 2.40 | 0.35 | 0.64 | 10.3 → 2.3 → 0.7 → 0.4 |
     | S&P | 2.35 | 0.48 | 0.88 | 25 → 5.3 → 3.2 → 3.3 |

     News H is 0.49.
   - **Robustness:** fat tails in 98%; memory beyond every no-chartist run in 46%; above 0.05 in 18%.
   - **EMH claims (calibrated):** mean log(p/pf) ≈ 0; more than 10% mispricing on 0.37% of days, longest stretch 11 days; volatility 0.67× (under 10% chartists) to 2.97× (over 90%).
   - **Final test status:** 511 passed, 4 xfailed (the 2 old reversal/memory ones, the dt one, the aggregation one).

## 2. How you want the rebuild done
**One honest limit first.** The Nature paper describes the model in words and shows its results, but it has **no equations and no parameter values**. Those are in its Supplementary Information and in Lux & Marchesi (2000), and we don't have either. So "rebuild with the paper" means:
- every **result** gets checked against the paper's text and Figs 1–3;
- the **equations** still come from the 2000 version, until you get the Supplementary Information through CMU's library. Getting it is the single most useful thing for this rebuild.

**My plan:** do it on a separate branch called `rebuild`, so `main` stays safe. Then build up again in steps. Each step has to pass its checks before the next one starts. This follows your rule: "smallest working version checked before adding features".

| Step | What gets rebuilt | Proof it's right |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Specification: a written list of every equation, assumption and paper figure, with its source | You read and approve it |

At the end, I'll give you a side-by-side comparison of the old and new versions, and `main` changes only if you approve.

## Lesson 1: Why this paper matters

On a normal day the S&P 500 moves by about **1%**. That typical size of a day's move is called the **standard deviation**. If daily moves followed a **bell curve** (the "normal distribution", where big surprises are extremely rare), a day many standard deviations away should essentially never happen. Yet on **Black Monday, October 19, 1987, the market fell about 20% in one day**, with no matching news that morning. Lux and Marchesi's paper asks whether traders copying each other could make such days by itself.

**Your first exercise:** if a normal day's move is about 1%, roughly how many standard deviations was Black Monday's −20% fall? And from what you know about the bell curve, would you call that rare, very rare, or "never in the age of the universe"?

## What the rebuild found
- **The rebuilt model reproduces the paper.** On held-out runs: tail α 2.62 (paper 2.64), H of returns 0.47 (0.48), H of move sizes 0.81 (0.85), and returns cross back over to a bell curve by 25 days.
- **Your proposal's checks pass.** With no chartists, kurtosis is 0.01, a bell curve. With herding, the tail exponent is about 3, like the S&P (2.92).
- **The S&P 500 looks like a typical model history on 9 of 11 measures.** It misses on two:
  - the model's returns reverse slightly day to day;
  - its tail is a bit thinner than the S&P's.
- **More herding is not always more crashes.** Tails are fattest at middling herding strength, near a tipping point, and turn back into a bell curve once herding gets strong. This is what the paper means by "criticality", and it's a good point for your TA check-in.

## Bugs found along the way
- **The old data files** had a mid-day price as their last row. The new download script stops every file on the same day, 30 Oct 2026.
- **The sources contradict each other** on how fast the price moves. Read literally, the price barely moves, so I followed the version the paper's theory relies on.
- **My first S&P fit gamed its own score.** It became wildly unstable so that every gap looked small. I fixed the scoring and refit.
- **My runaway guard was too strict.** It counted bubbles that burst on their own as endless bubbles.
- **The paper fit was overfitted** to 2 lucky seeds. Refitting on 8 seeds fixed it.

## What's still wrong
These are listed in `figures/RESULTS.md` and `KEY_POINTS.md`:
- the paper's own parameter values are in a paper we couldn't get, so both settings are fitted;
- after a big day the S&P bounces back and the model doesn't;
- the S&P-calibrated settings run away into an endless bubble in 2 of 40 simulated 400-year histories;
- the S&P keeps fatter tails over 25 days than the models do.

## Decisions for you
1. **Merge `rebuild` into `main`?**
2. **The circuit-breaker policy experiment wasn't rebuilt.** It was an extension, not part of the paper. Do you want it back?
3. **Get Lux & Marchesi (2000) through the CMU library.** Its parameter tables would let us replace the fitted settings with the real ones.

Next is the tutoring you asked for: the paper from the ground up, with the code and these figures in the lessons. Prompt suggestions are still off so they don't give away quiz answers. Say "start tutoring" when you're ready.

2. **Key Technical Concepts**

   **Model equations.** The Lux–Marchesi equations were rebuilt from Samanidou, Zschischang, Stauffer & Lux 2007 [S] (arXiv physics/0701140), cross-checked with Cristelli et al. 2011 [C] (arXiv 1101.1847):
   - Rates: π+− = v1(nc/N)e^U1; π+f = v2(n+/N)e^U21 (recruitment proportional to the destination group).
   - U1 = a1·x + (a2/v1)(dp/dt)/p.
   - U21 = a3[(r + ṗ/v2)/p − R − s|pf−p|/p], with r = R·pf.
   - U22 = a3[R − (r + ṗ/v2)/p − s|pf−p|/p].
   - ED_c = (n+ − n−)tc; ED_f = nf·γ(pf−p)/p.
   - Tick rate = max(0, ±β(ED+μ))/tick, so that on average d ln p/dt = β·ED (eq. 25). The tick is 0.1% of the price.
   - ṗ is measured over the last 0.1 day.
   - Floors of 4 traders in each group.
   - Exact switch probability 1 − exp(−rate·dt), with multinomial-style splitting.
   - Poisson ticks.
   - μ scaled by sqrt(0.01/dt).

   **Parameter set I** (remembered, unverified): N=500, v1=3, v2=2, β=6, tc=0.02, γ=0.01, a1=0.6, a2=0.2, a3=0.5, s=0.75, R=0.0004, σμ=0.05, σε=0.005. In our build it runs away (seed 1, day 28,511).

   **Runaway rule:** the price is more than e from its value for 250 days in a row (`MAX_DAYS`), or ever beyond e^20 (`HARD_GAP`). Bubbles that burst on their own are kept. The S&P fit uses `max_days=1`.

   **Fitting:**
   - Random search (1200 draws), then Nelder–Mead from the top 8.
   - Training seeds 100–107 (the paper fit used 100–101 at first, then was refined on all 8). Held-out seeds 1000–1039.
   - Paper loss: targets α_reg 2.64±0.15, H 0.48±0.03, H_abs 0.85±0.03, plus a penalty if kurt_25 > 1; 50k-day runs.
   - S&P loss: mean squared (median model − S&P)/tolerance, where the tolerance is a block bootstrap (250-day blocks, 200 draws). The first version divided by the model's own spread, which rewarded wild models; that bug was fixed.

   **Stats:**
   - fingerprint (11 measures): sd, kurtosis, alpha_hill, alpha_reg, ac_r_1, ac_abs_1, ac_abs_50, H, H_abs, kurt_5, kurt_25.
   - DFA (`dfa_scales`), Hill estimator, regression α.

   **Other:** numba JIT; multiprocessing Pool (needs `__main__` and a real script file); colorblind-validated palette (blue, orange, aqua via the dataviz validator); held-out-seed methodology; "typical" history = the median-mismatch one.

3. **Files and Code Sections**

   **Data**
   - `data/download.py` (new):
     - Uses yfinance with `auto_adjust=False`. `END="2026-10-30"`.
     - INDEXES: spx_daily (^GSPC 1950), spx_daily_1928, ndx (^NDX 1985), vix (^VIX 1990); 20 stocks from `stocks/market_beta.csv`.
     - FRED USREC fetched via urllib with a certifi SSL context.
     - `main(outdir)`.
   - All data files were replaced with the re-download. The old files' last day was a live mid-day price; every other day matched to within 1e-6. All 20 betas were reverified exactly.

   **`notes/SPEC.md`** (new): sources table [N]/[S]/[C]/[L]; state; sections 1–5 with equations; disagreements and the units choice; Runaways section; Fitting section (including the lesson learned); parameters; table of results to reproduce; project checks; allowed differences.

   **`herding/model.py`** (fully rewritten):
   - `SETTINGS` dict of 16 keys (N, v1, v2, a1, a2, a3, s, R, tc, gamma, beta, sigma_mu, sigma_eps, tick, dt, floor).
   - Constants: `SET_I`, `TREND_WINDOW=0.1`, `REFERENCE_DT=0.01`, `U_CAP=30`, `MAX_GAP=1.0`, `MAX_DAYS=250`, `HARD_GAP=20.0`, `START_CHARTISTS=0.1`.
   - `RunawayError(message, day)`.
   - `check(p)`: validates type, finiteness, non-negativity, positivity, whole numbers, s<1, tick<1, dt splitting a day, dt ≤ trend window, N ≥ 4·floor.
   - `_leave(g, n, rate_a, rate_b, dt)` and `_cap(u)`.
   - `_simulate(...)` takes `max_gap`, `max_days`, `hard_gap`; tracks `outside` days in a row; returns `out_lp, out_lf, out_z, out_x, done`.
   - `simulate(p, days, seed=None, chartists=True, max_gap=MAX_GAP, max_days=MAX_DAYS)` returns a dict with r, lp, lf, z, x, and raises RunawayError (overflow-safe message, ratio capped at exp(700)).

   **`herding/stats.py`:**
   - Kept the primitives (hill_alpha, kurtosis, ccdf, acf, tail_share, aggregate, alpha_regression, dfa, hurst).
   - Replaced `stylized_facts` with `dfa_scales(n, smallest=16, count=12)`, a `FINGERPRINT` dict and `fingerprint(r)` (needs ≥ 500 days).

   **`herding/calibrate.py`** (rewritten):
   - Constants: `SETTINGS_DIR=ROOT/"settings"`, `BURN=1000`, `TRAIN_SEEDS=100..107`, `HELD_OUT_SEEDS=1000..1039`.
   - `RANGES` (v1, v2 0.2–5 log; a1 0–1.2; a2 0–2; a3 0.1–3 log; tc 5e-4–0.05 log; gamma 2e-3–0.05 log; beta 1–20 log; sigma_mu 0–0.1). `SPX_RANGES` adds sigma_eps 0.002–0.02 log.
   - `PAPER_TARGETS`, `PAPER_KURT_25_MAX=1`, `PAPER_DAYS=50000`, `SPX_TARGETS`, `BOOTSTRAP_BLOCK=250`, `BOOTSTRAP_DRAWS=200`, `SPX_MAX_DAYS=1`, `RUNAWAY_LOSS=1e4`.
   - Functions:
     - `base`, `to_vector`, `from_vector` (clips), `draw`;
     - `histories(p, seeds, days, max_days)`, `ensemble`;
     - `paper_loss`, `block_bootstrap`, `spx_tolerance`, `spx_loss(fp, target, tol)` (uses the median);
     - `spx_target`, `fit_seeds(kind)`;
     - `class Fit(kind)` with `.ranges`, `.days`, `.seeds`, `.target`, `.tol`, `.max_days`, `loss`, `score_draw`, `loss_of_vector`;
     - `_refine`, `run(kind, draws=1200, keep=8, iters=150, workers=8)`;
     - `class _PoolFit(Fit)`, `_fingerprint_one`, `refine_saved(kind, iters=200)`;
     - `save(kind, loss, p, seeds=None)`, `load(kind)`.
   - CLI: `python3 -m herding.calibrate paper|spx [--refine]`.

   **`settings/paper.json`:** loss 0.061, train_seeds 100–107. v1 1.9068, v2 1.1574, a1 0.5355, a2 1.5765, a3 2.2094, tc 0.001 (Tc 0.48), gamma 0.0342 (Tf 17.12), beta 1.1922, sigma_mu 0.0157, sigma_eps 0.005.

4. **Errors and fixes**

   **Data download**
   - FRED SSL certificate error: fixed with a certifi context.

   **Model and units**
   - Tick units: eq. 24 taken literally gave a frozen price (35% zero-return days). Fixed by dividing the tick rate by the tick size (eq. 25).
   - μ effect depended on dt: fixed by scaling μ with sqrt(0.01/dt).
   - Runaway guard too strict: it flagged bubbles that burst on their own (4/40 held-out). Changed to a persistence rule; the S&P fit stays strict (`max_days=1`).
   - Overflow in the RunawayError message: the ratio is capped.

   **Fitting**
   - Paper fit overfitting: the 2-seed fit gave α 2.84 held-out. Added `--refine` on all 8 seeds: α 2.62.
   - `save()` recorded the wrong seeds: fixed with `fit_seeds`.
   - S&P loss rewarded wild models (1000-day stretches of 10% daily moves; kurtosis rising with τ): switched to the bootstrap tolerance and median, then refit.
   - Overflow warning in a calibrate test with 1e3 log coordinates: changed to 50.

   **Mistakes in tests and scripts**
   - Test mistakes: dt=0.2 expected the wrong message; a tiny v2 without a3=0 blows up U2.
   - Bursting-bubble test used rounded settings: switched to the exact values.
   - A lost parametrize decorator in `test_stats` was restored.
   - DFA scales were too large in the probe script.
   - The efficiency script's empty quantile selection produced NaN: fixed with `<=`.

   **Figures**
   - Fig 2b kurtosis 1925 from pooled histories: now the per-history median.
   - Fig 4: overlapping labels and symlog −10⁰ fixed.
   - Fig 7: outlier squashing fixed.
   - Fig 9: legend overlap and an overstated title fixed.
   - Fig 1 legend and Fig 3 caption typo fixed.
   - Fig 5 axis range fixed.

   **Repo**
   - `scratch/old_tests/test_model.py` had been git-tracked: removed with `git rm --cached`.

**1. Merge into `main`:** done on your computer only. Local `main` now matches `rebuild` (it was a fast-forward, so no new commit). Pushing `main` to GitHub was blocked by a permission check, so GitHub's `main` still has the old version.

**2. Circuit breakers:** rebuilt with tests.
- **The rules:** the model now has an optional version of the real US rules. Trading pauses for 15 minutes at −7% and −13% below the previous close, and closes for the day at −20%. With the breakers off, the model's output is exactly the same as before.
- **The one-time shock:** the experiment also drops the stock's true value by 10% at once, standing in for a surprise like an unexpected rate hike.
- **The comparison:** each history is run twice with the same random numbers, once with breakers and once without. The two runs stay identical until the first pause, so that day is the fair comparison.
- **First result** (with the old S&P settings; it will be redone with the new fit):
  - **Breakers make no measurable difference.** The same-day change differs by −0.1 percentage points, and the 95% range of the estimate runs from −0.3 to +0.1.
  - **The 10% shock causes bigger overshoots when more traders are chartists.** When fewer than 5% are chartists, the price falls about the 10% the value fell. At 10–20% chartists the drop overshoots noticeably. I measure the overshoot against the same history without the shock, so ordinary day-to-day wobble isn't counted as overshoot.
- **New files:** `herding/policy.py`, Fig 11 in `make_figures.py`, `tests/test_policy.py` and a "Policy experiments" section in `notes/SPEC.md`.
- **The tests caught three of my mistakes, all fixed:**
  - a bug that measured the "first pause" after the two runs had already split apart. It had made breakers look harmful;
  - a "calm" test market that was actually 64% chartists;
  - a test that assumed the price moves only a few ticks per time step.

**3. Recalibration:** running in the background.
- **What changed:** each fit now tries 3,000 random settings instead of 1,200. It then runs each finalist for 100,000 days (about 400 years) on 20 new seeds and keeps the best one that doesn't run away into an endless bubble. The old S&P settings did that in 2 of 40 long runs.
- **Progress:** the paper fit is about 45 minutes in, and the S&P fit runs after it. In total it could take a few more hours.
- **When it finishes,** I'll compare the new settings with the old ones (copies are in `scratch/recalibration/before/`). Then I'll remake all the figures, update `RESULTS.md` and `KEY_POINTS.md`, and run the full test suite. The quick tests all pass after the fixes above.

**The plan, in order:**
1. **Finish the current work:** the new fits, the comparison with the old settings, all 11 figures redrawn, `RESULTS.md` and `KEY_POINTS.md` updated, and the full test suite run.
2. **Three checkers at the same time.** None of them can change anything:
   - **Independent auditor.** It gets only the data, the equations and the code, not my summaries or conclusions. It recomputes the key numbers itself and checks the code against the equations and the figures against the numbers.
   - **Data checker.** It compares the files in `data/` with the original sources (Yahoo Finance, FRED), and checks that the equations match the papers they come from.
   - **Efficiency checker.** It looks for slow, repeated or overcomplicated code and suggests fixes.
3. **One fixer per problem,** taking the problems one at a time so their edits don't collide. Each fixer writes a test that shows its problem, then fixes it.
4. **A re-checker after every fix.** It reruns the tests and confirms that the fix works and broke nothing else.
5. **A final full rundown.** One last agent reruns everything from scratch: tests, `main.py`, the figures and the document numbers. It reports what stands and what doesn't.

**Three rules for the agents:**
- **No fix may change the model's output or the fitted settings without your approval.** That could mean hours of refitting, so agents will report such fixes, not apply them.
- **Agents don't commit.** I'll ask you for a commit message at the end.
- **About 8–10 agents in total.** If the checkers find more problems than that, I'll fix the most serious ones and list the rest for you.

**Claude:** You're right, I'd guessed instead of checking the last run's real times. I've looked now, and found the actual cause of the slowdown.

**Your Mac is on battery with Low Power Mode on.** It's an M1 running at 94% battery. Low Power Mode slows the processor down, so each of the 8 fit workers gets only about 42% of a core. That's why this fit is running about 4 times slower than your last one, which scored 1,200 settings in 305 seconds.

## The fit (12:15 pm)
- **It sped up a lot on the charger.** The 8 candidates being polished went from 31–78 scored steps to 37–89 in one minute, out of 150 each.

## The tests
- **Count:** 187 test functions, about 360 individual test cases once each one is repeated for its different inputs.
- **Last quick run:** everything passed after the fixes below. That run covers all tests except the slow ones.
- **Added in this session (29 new tests):**
  - **Circuit breakers (17):**
    - breakers that never fire change nothing;
    - a day without a pause never fell as far as the first level;
    - after a close, the price stays frozen;
    - a pause lasting the whole day freezes the close between the two levels;
    - the minutes-to-steps conversion;
    - a one-time drop in the value moves it by exactly its size;
    - rejecting bad inputs.
  - **Policy experiments (14):**
    - in a market without herding, a 10% drop moves the price by exactly 10%;
    - the "first pause" is measured from two identical histories;
    - the overshoot measure matches a hand calculation;
    - the 95% range is the expected width.
  - **Calibration (5):**
    - the stability check puts a stable but worse fit ahead of an unstable better one;
    - the progress saving resumes correctly after a line cut off mid-write;
    - a stopped polish gives the identical answer after a restart.
- **Mistakes the tests caught, all fixed:**
  - a real bug where a cut-off line in the progress file also lost the next saved result;
  - two wrong assumptions in my own tests: a "calm" test market that was actually 64% chartists, and a test that assumed one time step moves only a few ticks.
- **One more bug I found by re-reading the code, not through a test:** the "first pause" comparison had been measuring runs that had already split apart during the warm-up days. It had made breakers look harmful.

## Recalibration result
I kept the old settings for both fits, because neither refit was better where it counts.

| | Old settings | New settings | Kept |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Paper-matched** | training score 0.061, never ran away | 0.554, never ran away | **old** (lower is better) |
| **S&P-calibrated** | 2.94; S&P typical on **9 of 11** held-out measures | 1.51, never ran away, but S&P typical on only **6 of 11** | **old** |

- **Why the new S&P fit lost:** it had thin tails (kurtosis 4 against the S&P's 25) and almost no herding (a1 = 0.18, against 1.08 before).
- **Why the fit picked it anyway:** the fit's score measures each gap in units of how uncertain the S&P's own number is. The S&P's kurtosis is very uncertain, because it hinges on Black Monday, so missing the fat tails cost the score almost nothing.
- **This changes your choice of score.** Fixing the score to weigh the tails more would mean another 2-hour fit, so I've put that question on the list below. The rejected fit is saved in `scratch/recalibration/`.
- **Checks:** all **341 tests pass**, and figures 1–10 came out identical pixel for pixel. RESULTS.md and KEY_POINTS.md now cover the recalibration and the new Fig 11.

## Bugs fixed since the last update
- Fig 11 crashed when no breaker fired, which happens in short test runs; the slow figure test caught it.
- A test of mine read the wrong number out of Fig 11's summary.
- I had written in RESULTS.md that the kept S&P settings never run away in long runs. That came from a run with the *new* settings. I reran it: the kept settings run away in **2 of 40**, and the documents now say so.

## The review, now running
- **Three checkers at the same time:**
  - an **independent auditor** who reads only the data, the equations and the code, recomputes the numbers itself, and only then compares them with the documents;
  - a **data checker** who downloads the data again from Yahoo and FRED and compares the equations with the papers they come from;
  - an **efficiency checker**.
- **Fixes:** one fixer per problem, for up to the 6 most serious problems that don't change any results. Each fixer is followed by its own re-checker.
- **Final rundown:** one agent reruns everything from scratch.
- **Undoing:** a restore point is saved, so any fix can be reversed exactly.
- **Rules:** problems whose fix would change results are reported, not fixed. No agent commits anything.

The Mac stays awake for up to 3 hours. I'll report when the review is done.

3. Do you want a refit with a score that weighs the fat tails more? That's about 2 hours.
4. Should I stop tracking the large journal `.jsonl` file in git? It's 88 MB, and GitHub refuses files over 100 MB.

**Claude:** Everything you asked for is done: all **366 tests pass**, `main.py` still finds the S&P typical on 9 of 11 measures, and nothing is committed yet.

## 1. Fixes that change results
- **Circuit breakers now follow the real 3:25 pm rule.**
  - **The rule:** a 7% or 13% fall after 3:25 pm no longer pauses trading, but a 20% fall still closes the market at any time.
  - **The new result:** pauses now fire about 4 times per 77-year history instead of 5. The conclusion holds: no detectable effect. On the day of the first pause the difference is −0.16 percentage points (95% interval −0.42 to +0.08). None of the 200 breaker histories ran away (before: 2).
- **Fig 8's stocks now get their own seeds.**
  - **Unusual cells:** 43 of 220 instead of 49.
  - **The story changed:** stocks still have higher kurtosis (median percentile 95), but their tail exponents are now typical. Their clearest miss is H: 11 of 20 stocks don't reverse from day to day the way the model does.
- **Fig 10:** points resting on a single surviving history are no longer drawn. The text now says there's a lower limit of herding strength, not a "narrow band". It also says the herding peak has two possible causes the figure can't tell apart: chartists locking into one mood, or there being fewer of them.

## 2. Quick fixes (19 of the 20)
- **13 wording and source-credit fixes in RESULTS.md, KEY_POINTS.md and SPEC.md.** For example:
  - the "mu is Gaussian" assumption was credited to sources that don't say it;
  - the floor of 4 traders is this project's own choice;
  - the inverse cubic law comes from 5-minute stock returns, not daily index returns.
- **The stocks README** now explains that the betas were measured over 2005–2025, a narrower window than the data.
- **Speed-ups:**
  - Fig 9's baseline runs now use all 8 workers;
  - the stability check runs all candidates in one batch;
  - the slow test uses a real worker pool;
  - one shared test helper replaces 4 copies;
  - 82 stale numba cache files are deleted.
- **Fig 9 came out identical pixel for pixel,** so these speed-ups changed no results.
- **Four optional speed-ups skipped** because they need restructuring: a disk cache, reusing histories across scripts, and two smaller ones. The 20th issue, one script simulating the same 5 histories twice, I left: it costs about 2 seconds.

## 3. What to pull from Bloomberg
Bloomberg's license forbids sharing its raw data and your repo is public. So keep any Bloomberg files in a git-ignored folder and commit only the statistics you compute from them. In order of value:

1. **Economic-release surprises, to test finding 11 against real data.** The model predicts a surprise moves the price more when the market is crowded. Bloomberg's economic calendar (`ECO`) keeps, for each Fed decision and CPI release, the economists' median forecast and the actual number. Their difference is the "surprise". Pull that history for Fed decisions, CPI and jobs reports, plus the S&P 500 (`SPX Index`) the same day.
2. **A real-world stand-in for "share of chartists".**
   - the CBOE put/call ratio, a measure of how heavily investors are betting on falls;
   - the AAII investor sentiment survey;
   - the CFTC's report on how speculators are positioned in S&P 500 futures.

   Search for each on the terminal, since I'm not sure of their exact codes. Together with item 1, this tests whether surprises hit harder when the crowd leans one way. It also tests the model's mechanism directly: more crowding should mean more volatility.
3. **Minute-by-minute S&P 500 prices on the days circuit breakers fired:** 9, 12, 16 and 18 March 2020, plus 27 October 1997. They would show what really happened after trading reopened, which is the weakest assumption in Fig 11. Check first how far back the terminal keeps minute data: on many terminals it's only about 6 months, and if so these days won't be there.
4. **Lower priority:**
   - S&P 500 earnings and dividend history, as a real "value" to compare the price against;
   - `SPXT Index`, the S&P 500 including dividends, since Yahoo's prices leave dividends out.

Bloomberg won't have the Lux & Marchesi (2000) parameter tables; those still come from the CMU library. If you only have time for one visit, pull items 1 and 2.

2. Key Technical Concepts:

   **Model and Lux–Marchesi equations** (unchanged): see `notes/SPEC.md`. Numba JIT, multiprocessing Pool.

   **Circuit breakers** (US NYSE Rule 7.12, formerly 80B, SEC-approved):
   - 15-minute pauses at 7% and 13% only before 3:25 pm (minute 355 of 390, step 91 of 100); close at 20% at any time.
   - Halted ticks are dropped, not queued.
   - Levels are scaled by model sd / S&P sd (×0.906).
   - Paired runs: on/off share random numbers until the first halt; first-halt event comparison at `EVENT_DAYS = (1, 5, 20)`.

   **Policy surprise:**
   - The value drops 10% via `shocks={day: -0.1}`.
   - Ratio = 1 − (Δ(lp − lf)) / |ln 0.9|, with a placebo from the same seed without the shock.
   - Paired overshoot uses `policy.median_overshoot` (added by fixer 6).

   **Calibration:**
   - Stability screen: `STABILITY_SEEDS` 2000–2019, `STABILITY_DAYS` 100,000; candidates sorted by (runaways, loss).
   - `--draws N`.
   - Checkpointing (`checkpointed`, JSONL, resume; handles a cut-off last line).
   - `SavedLoss` caches every Nelder–Mead evaluation so a restarted run replays deterministically.
   - `CHECKPOINT_DIR` = `scratch/calibrate`; checkpoint files are deleted after success.

   **Recalibration lesson:** the SPX bootstrap-tolerance loss can trade away fat tails, because the S&P's kurtosis uncertainty is huge. The new SPX fit (loss 1.508, a1 0.18, Tc 0.27) was rejected: only 6/11 held-out measures typical.

   **Seeds:**

   | Use | Seeds |
   |---|---|
   | Training | 100–107 |
   | Held-out | 1000–1039 (Fig 8: indexes 1000–1019, stocks 1020–1039) |
   | Stability screen | 2000–2019 |
   | Breakers | 3000–3199 |
   | Surprises | 4000–4399 |

   **Other:**
   - Bootstrap CI via `paired_difference`.
   - Fig 10 `MIN_SURVIVORS = 5`.
   - Pixel-identity checks of figures using `imread` + `array_equal`.
   - Workflow tool (16 agents).
   - `caffeinate -i -s -w PID`.
   - Low Power Mode slows fits about 4×.

3. Files and Code Sections:

     ```python
     MINUTES_PER_DAY = 390
     ```
   - `_simulate(..., hard_gap, cb1, cb2, cb3, halt_steps, pause_end, shock)` returns `out_lp, out_lf, out_z, out_x, halts, done`.
   - Loop: `for k in range(steps)`. A shock is applied at day start (`lf += shock[day]`), and `prev_close` is recorded.
   - Halt logic:
     ```python
     if halts[day] == 3 or paused > 0: paused = max(0, paused - 1)
     else: (poisson ticks); fall = lp - prev_close
       if fall <= cb3: halts[day] = 3
       elif fall <= cb2 and halts[day] < 2 and k < pause_end: halts[day]=2; paused=halt_steps
       elif fall <= cb1 and halts[day] < 1 and k < pause_end: halts[day]=1; paused=halt_steps
     ```
   - `check_breakers(breakers)`: `levels` and `halt_minutes` are required; `last_pause_minute` is optional (0–390).
   - `check_shocks(shocks, days)`; `pause_steps(minutes, dt) = max(1, round(minutes/390/dt))`; `minute_to_step(minute, dt) = round(minute/390/dt)`; `_is_number`.
   - `simulate(p, days, seed=None, chartists=True, max_gap=MAX_GAP, max_days=MAX_DAYS, breakers=None, shocks=None)` returns a dict that includes `halts`.
   - With breakers off, output is identical (the golden test passes).

   **`herding/policy.py`** (new):
   - `scaled_breakers(model_sd, market_sd, rules=US_BREAKERS)` returns `dict(rules, levels=scaled), scale`, so `last_pause_minute` is kept.
   - `crash_measures(r, crash_level)`.
   - `EVENT_DAYS`; `_breaker_pair` (first halt of the whole run, warm-up included).
   - `breaker_experiment(pool, p, seeds, days, rules)` → `(whole, events, runaways)`.
   - `_surprise_one` (peak, end, placebo_peak, chartists); `surprise_experiment(pool, p, seeds, size=-0.10, horizon=10)`.
   - `paired_difference(off, on, draws=2000, seed=0)`; `median_overshoot` (fixer 6).
   - Imports `BURN` and `worst_fall` from `experiments`.

   **`herding/calibrate.py`:**
   - Constants: `STABILITY_SEEDS`, `STABILITY_DAYS`, `CHECKPOINT_DIR`.
   - Functions: `_runs_away`; `runaways(pool, p, seeds, days)`; `screen(pool, candidates, ...)`, which runs one `pool.map` over all candidate × seed jobs with `chunksize=1`.
   - Checkpointing: `checkpointed(pool, fn, jobs, path)`, `SavedLoss(fn, path)`, and `_refine(args)` with an optional 4th arg holding the score-cache path.
   - `run(kind, draws=1200, keep=8, iters=150, workers=8, checkpoint_dir=CHECKPOINT_DIR)` returns `(loss, p, bad)`; `refine_saved` also screens.
   - `save(kind, loss, p, seeds=None, runaways=None, draws=None)` stores stability info.
   - CLI accepts `--draws`.
   - The paper-error-bar comment now gives the numbers inline; the `PAPER_ERROR_BARS` constant was removed.

   **`herding/stats.py`:** `tail_share` removed.

4. Errors and fixes:

   **Permissions and power:**
   - Pushing to `main` was blocked by the classifier at first. The local `main` was fast-forwarded instead. Later the user explicitly asked to push all, and `git push origin rebuild:main` succeeded.
   - The fit ran slowly: Low Power Mode on battery made it about 4× slower. The user turned Low Power Mode off and plugged in; I used `caffeinate` to keep the machine awake.
   - The fit's output was buffered and showed no progress. Later runs use `python3 -u`.

   **Bugs in my own code:**
   - The first-halt event was measured after the warm-up, so the runs might already have diverged. Fixed to use the first halt of the whole run.
   - The surprise's paired price comparison was noisy because news diverges between the paired runs. Switched to a gap-based measure plus a placebo.
   - The checkpoint glued a new line onto a cut-off last line. Fixed by writing a newline first.
   - `scaled_breakers` dropped `last_pause_minute`. Fixed with `dict(rules, levels=...)`.
   - Fig 11 crashed with 0 events, caught by the slow test. Fixed with NaN diffs.
   - The Fig 10 caption was missing its f-string prefix.
   - A false claim in RESULTS that the kept SPX settings had "0 of 40" runaways. Rerun `stability.py`: 2 of 40; corrected.

   **Mistaken test assumptions:**
   - The "calm" market in a test was 64% chartists; fixed with `tc=0`.
   - A tick-step assumption failed with set I; fixed with a `beta=0.05` market.
   - The `surprise_` key filter also caught `surprise_runaways`.
   - The cutoff test compared `halts`, which records only each day's maximum level; it now compares prices.

**Claude:** The first result is in. The **tail-weighted fit does not beat the current settings**:

| | Current settings | Tail-weighted fit |
|---|---|---|
| Measures typical, out of 11 | 9 | 8 |
| Held-out loss | 4.15 | 5.82 |
| Long runs that ran away | 2 | 0 |

This is a trade-off, not a free improvement:

- **What it fixed:** the fat tails. Kurtosis and both tail exponents are now typical. The paper-style tail exponent was unusual before.
- **What it lost:** the long memory in volatility, meaning calm and wild periods lasting a long time. The S&P sits at the very edge of the model's range on both memory measures.
- **What it did to herding:** a1, the strength of herding, dropped from 1.08 to 0.09.

So far, no single setting matches both the tails and the long memory. That's worth saying in your writeup. I've added this to `scratch/overnight/MORNING.md`.

Next in the queue are the differential-evolution search on the S&P, then the 1928 fit, then the differential-evolution search on the paper fit.

**Claude:** The final review finished at 8:20. It found no bugs, the 15 numbers it rechecked all match, all 13 figures look right, and the quick tests pass (358 run, plus 8 slow ones skipped, for 366 total). Here are the 9 questions waiting for you, each with my suggestion:

**Decisions about the results**
1. **Switch to the differential-evolution S&P settings?** They have 1 fewer typical measure (8 vs 9 of 11). But their held-out loss is much lower (2.78 vs 4.15) and they never ran away (0 vs 2 of 20). *My suggestion:* keep the current settings, because that is the rule we fixed before seeing any results. Changing the rule after seeing them would weaken your conclusions.
2. **Should RESULTS.md argue for switching, or only report it?** *My suggestion:* only report it. Mention it as an honest "close second" with its trade-offs.
3. **Is the 3× weight on tail measures in the tail-weighted fit OK?** It was my choice, not the paper's. *My suggestion:* keep it, and say in the write-up that the 3 is arbitrary.

**Figures**
4. **Fig 9a:** keep the x axis running to 5.5, which leaves empty space on the right, or move the legend outside the plot? *My suggestion:* move it outside; empty space wastes the plot.
5. **Fig 6b:** its settings break Kirman's "either/or" story when read literally, because the two chances add up to more than 1. Switch to settings like δ = 0.1, or keep it with the code-comment note? *My suggestion:* switch to δ = 0.1, so the figure matches the story you'd tell in a presentation.

**Small things**
6. **H of |returns|:** should the docs say ± 0.03 instead of ± 0.02? The measured spread is 0.027. *My suggestion:* yes, ± 0.03.
7. **An 8% speed-up that isn't guaranteed to give bit-identical results:** accept it? *My suggestion:* no. 8% isn't worth losing exact reproducibility.

**Git**
8. **What commit message do you want** for tonight's work? It needs to be in your own words.
9. **Merge `overnight` into `main`** after committing? *My suggestion:* yes, once you've looked at the new figures.
